Chapter 5

Modeling by Genetic Algorithm

## 5.1 Introduction

Genetic algorithms are one of the best ways to solve a problem for which little is known. They are a very general algorithm and so will work well in any search space. All you need to know is what you need the solution to be able to do well, and a genetic algorithm will be able to create a high quality solution. Genetic algorithms use the principles of selection and evolution to produce several solutions to a given problem.

5.2 Biological Background

Every organism has a set of rules, a blueprint so to speak, describing how that organism is built up from the tiny building blocks of life. These rules are encoded in the genes of an organism, which in turn are connected together into long strings called chromosome

![fig5.1.jpg](images/00044.jpeg)

When two organisms mate they share their genes. The resultant offspring may end up having half the genes from one parent and half from the other (fig.5.1). This process is called recombination.

5.3 Method

Genetic algorithms tend to thrive in an environment in which there is a very large set of candidate solutions and in which the search space is uneven and has many hills and valleys. True, genetic algorithms will do well in any environment, but they will be greatly outclassed by more situation specific algorithms in the simpler search spaces. Therefore you must keep in mind that genetic algorithms are not always the best choice. Sometimes they can take quite a while to run and are therefore not always feasible for real time use. They are, however, one of the most powerful methods with which to (relatively) quickly create high quality solutions to a problem. Now, before we start, I'm going to provide you with some key terms so that this article makes sense.

*   Individual - Any possible solution
*   Population - Group of all individuals
*   Search Space - All possible solutions to the problem
*   Chromosome - Blueprint for an individual
*   Trait - Possible aspect of an individual
*   Allele - Possible settings for a trait
*   Locus - The position of a gene on the chromosome
*   Genome - Collection of all chromosomes for an individual

A typical chromosome may look like this:

100101011101010010100

The most common type of genetic algorithm works like this: a population is created with a group of individuals created randomly. The individuals in the population are then evaluated. The evaluation function is provided by the programmer and gives the individuals a score based on how well they perform at the given task. Two individuals are then selected based on their fitness, the higher the fitness, the higher the chance of being selected. These individuals then "reproduce" to create one or more offspring, after which the offspring are mutated randomly. This continues until a suitable solution has been found or a certain number of generations have passed, depending on the needs of the programmer.

### 5.3.1 Selection

While there are many different types of selection, I will cover the most common type - roulette wheel selection. In roulette wheel selection, individuals are given a probability of being selected that is directly proportionate to their fitness. Two individuals are then chosen randomly based on these probabilities and produce offspring. Pseudo-code for a roulette wheel selection algorithm is shown below(fig 5.2).

![fig5.2.jpg](images/00045.jpeg)

Imagine that the population’s total fitness score is represented by a pie chart, or roulette wheel. Now you assign a slice of the wheel to each member of the population. The size of the slice is proportional to that chromosomes fitness score. i.e. the fitter a member is the bigger the slice of pie it gets. Now, to choose a chromosome all you have to do is spin the ball and grab the chromosome at the point it stop

While this code is very general and will obviously not compile, it illustrates the basic structure of a selection algorithm. Besides, you should write the code yourself, you learn better that way.

### 5.3.2 Crossover

![fig5.3.jpg](images/00046.jpeg)

So now you have selected your individuals, and you know that you are supposed to somehow produce offspring with them, but how should you go about doing it? The most common solution is something called crossover, and while there are many different kinds of crossover, the most common type is single point crossover. In single point crossover, you choose a locus at which you swap the remaining alleles from on parent to the other. This is complex and is best understood visually.

As you can see, the children take one section of the chromosome from each parent. The point at which the chromosome is broken depends on the randomly selected crossover point. This particular method is called single point crossover because only one crossover point exists. Sometimes only child 1 or child 2 is created, but oftentimes both offspring are created and put into the new population. Crossover does not always occur, however. Sometimes, based on a set probability, no crossover occurs and the parents are copied directly to the new population. The probability of crossover occurring is usually 60% to 70%.

### 5.3.3 Mutation

After selection and crossover, you now have a new population full of individuals. Some are directly copied, and others are produced by crossover. In order to ensure that the individuals are not all exactly the same, you allow for a small chance of mutation. You loop through all the alleles of all the individuals, and if that allele is selected for mutation, you can either change it by a small amount or replace it with a new value. The probability of mutation is usually between 1 and 2 tenths of a percent. A visual for mutation is shown (Fig 5.4).

![fig5.4.jpg](images/00047.jpeg)

As you can easily see, mutation is fairly simple. You just change the selected alleles based on what you feel is necessary and move on. Mutation is, however, vital to ensuring genetic diversity within the population.

5.3.4 Steps of Algorithm

Algorithm consists of following steps.

1.  Generate random population of n chromosomes (suitable solutions for the problem)
2.  Evaluate the fitness f(x) of each chromosome x in the population

3.  Create a new population by repeating following steps:

a. Selection: Select two parent chromosomes from a population according to their fitness. In roulette wheel selection, individuals are given a probability of being selected that is directly proportionate to their fitness  (the better fitness, the bigger chance to be selected)

b. Crossover: With a crossover probability cross over the parents to form a new offspring. The most common type is single point crossover .If no crossover was performed; offspring is an exact copy of parents.

c. Mutation: With a mutation probability mutate new offspring at each locus (position in chromosome).

d. Accepting: Place new offspring in a new population

4.  Use new generated population for a further run of algorithm
5.  Test If the end condition is satisfied, stop, and return the best solution in current population or else, (Loop) Go to step 2

## 5.4 Applications

Genetic algorithms are a very effective way of quickly finding a reasonable solution to a complex problem. Granted they aren't instantaneous, or even close, but they do an excellent job of searching through a large and complex search space. Genetic algorithms are most effective in a search space for which little is known. You may know exactly what you want a solution to do but have no idea how you want it to go about doing it. This is where genetic algorithms thrive. They produce solutions that solve the problem in ways you may never have even considered. Then again, they can also produce solutions that only work within the test environment and flounder once you try to use them in the real world. Put simply: use genetic algorithms for everything you cannot easily do with another algorithm. Genetic Algorithms has been widely applied in many fields in engineering worlds. Not only does GAs provide an alternative method to solving problem, it consistently outperforms other traditional methods in most of the problems. It is one of the best ways to solve a problem for which little is known. Algorithm is started with a set of solutions (represented by chromosomes) called population. Solutions from one population are taken and used to form a new population to find better than the old one. Solutions that are selected to form new solutions (offspring) are selected according to their fitness - the more suitable they are the more chances they have to reproduce.

Chapter 6

Modeling by Artificial Neural Network

6.1 Artificial neural network (ANN)

An artificial neural network (ANN) is a non linear statistical data modeling operating system that can be used to model complex relationship between input and  output variables, through training, using huge number of experimentally generated data. NNs are capable of generalizing experience obtained in the course of learning. As a result, it becomes possible to simulate the situations, which are absent in the set of experimental results.  Fig 1 illustrates how output is computed from sets of input.

![fig6.1.jpg](images/00049.jpeg)

6.2 Method

The steps in Artificial neural network (ANN)  as follows.

ANN

• Receives Inputs X1 X2 …Xp from experimental data or real data

• Inputs fed-in through connections with ‘weights’

• Total Input = Weighted sum of  inputs from all sources

• Transfer function (Activation function) converts the input to output

• Output goes to other neurons or environment

There  can be three types of transfer function as shown in fig6.2\. The ANN consists of  (i) architecture of the neural network, (ii) training algorithm, and (iii) transfer function. The term “architecture of the neural network” refers to the number of the layers in the NN , that is input,  hidden and  output layers  and as shown in fig 6.3.. The numbers of neurons in the input layer and the output layer are determined by the numbers of input and output parameters, respectively. In order to find the optimal architecture, different numbers of neurons in the hidden layer are attempted (fig 6.3). At the input layer, each of neutrons has certain informative inputs which are equal to the number of independent input parameters of the system, and a certain number of outputs.  The output parameter is calculated by substituting a set of input parameters into a certain function (choice of transfer function fig2)  taking into account the corresponding set of weight coefficients. In order to find the optimal architecture, different numbers of neurons in the hidden layer are attempted.

![fig6.2.jpg](images/00050.jpeg)

6.2.1 Training

ANN needs training to operate and is capable of generalizing experience obtained in the course of learning. Usually, neural networks are trained using a large number of input with corresponding output data (input/output pairs) so that a particular set of inputs produces, as nearly as possible, a specific set of target outputs, by adjusting the weight associated with each connection (synapse) between neurons. As a result, it becomes possible to predict the situations, which are absent in the set of experimental results.

Given the Architecture There are  weights to decide.

W = (W1, W2,  …, W8)

Training Data: (Yi , X1i, X2i, …, Xpi )   i= 1,2,…,n

Given a particular choice of W, Y’s are predicted as  ( V1,V2,…,Vn)

They are function of W.

Wis chosen such that over all prediction, the error E is minimizedfrom the following eqn.

      E =  S (Yi – Vi) 2

![fig6.3.jpg](images/00051.jpeg)

There are many different training algorithms. The details for the NN training algorithms are given in [9]. In order to achieve the best result, different training algorithms were attempted including Batch Gradient Descent, Batch Gradient Descent with Momentum, One-step-secant, Scaled Conjugate Gradient, Resilient Back propagation, Variable Learning Rate, and Levenberg-Marquardt. Fig 6.4 illustrates feed forward and back propagation method.

The steps are as follows

• Start with a random set of weights.

• Feed forward the first observation through the net X1Network V1 ; Error  = (Y1 – V1)

• Adjust the weights so that this error is reduced   ( network fits the first observation well )

• Feed forward the second observation.
  Adjust weights to fit the second observation well

• Keep repeating till you reach the last observation This finishes one CYCLE through the data

• Perform many such training cycles till the   overall prediction error E is small.

6.2.2 Back propagation method

In the Back propagation method, Each weight ‘Shares the Blame’ for prediction error with other weights. Back Propagation algorithm decides how to distribute the blame among all weights and adjust the weights accordingly. Small portion of blame leads to small adjustment.  Large portion of the blame leads to large adjustment. The weight adjustment formula in Back Propagation are as follows

Vi – the prediction for ith observation – is a function of the network weights vector W = ( W1, W2,….)

Hence, E,  the total prediction error is also a function of W E( W) =  S [ Yi – Vi( W ) ] 2

6.2.3 Gradient Descent Method :

For every individual weight Wi, updation formula looks like

Wnew = Wold + a * ( ¶E / ¶W) |Wold ,a = Learning Parameter (between 0 and 1)

Another slight variation is also used sometimes

W(t+1) = W(t) + a * ( ¶E / ¶W) |W(t) + b * (W(t) - W(t-1) ),b = Momentum (between 0 and 1)

• Plot E vs ( w1, w2 ) - a 3-D surface -  ‘Error Surface’

• Aim is to identify that pair for which E is minimum

• That means – identify the pair for which the height of the error surface is minimum.

Gradient Descent Algorithm

•   Start with a random point ( w1, w2 )

• Move to a ‘better’ point ( w’1, w’2 ) where the height of error surface is lower.

Keep moving till you reach ( w*1, w*2 ), where the error is minimum

![fig6.4.jpg](images/00052.jpeg)

![fig6.5.jpg](images/00053.jpeg)

Chapter 7

Finite Element Method (FEM)

7.1 Basic Principles

Consider a body or engineering component through which the distribution of a field variable, e.g. displacement or stress, is required. Examples could be a component under load, temperatures subject to a heat input, etc. The body, i.e. a one-, two- or three-dimensional solid, is modelled as being hypothetically subdivided into an assembly of small parts called elements – ‘finite elements’. The word ‘finite’ is used to describe the limited, or finite, number of degrees of freedom used to model the behavior of each element. The elements are assumed to be connected to one another, but only at interconnected joints, known as nodes. It is important to note that the elements are notionally small regions, not separate entities like bricks, and there are no cracks or surfaces between them. (There are systems available that do model materials and structures comprising actual discrete elements such as real masonry bricks, particle mixes, grains of sand, etc., but these are outside the scope of this course.)

The complete set, or assemblage of elements, is known as a mesh. The process of representing a component as an assemblage of finite elements, known as discretisation, is the first of many key steps in understanding the FEM of analysis. An example is illustrated in Figure 1\. This is a plate-type component modelled with a number of mostly rectangular elements with a uniform thickness (into the page or screen) that could be, say, 2 mm.

![fig7.1.jpg](images/00055.jpeg)

7.2 How to do FEM

The number and type of elements chosen must be such that the variable distribution through the whole body is adequately approximated by the combined elemental representations. For example, if the mesh is too coarse, the resolution of the parametric distribution may be inadequate, whereas too fine a mesh is wasteful of computing time and possibly the user’s time, and in some cases, won’t even solve anyway. Part of the skill will be in designing and refining meshes in areas of high interest or concentration of results variation gradients.

7.2\. 1 Dividing Body into Elements

After model discretisation, i.e. subdividing the model domain into discrete elements (the mesh), the governing equations for each element are calculated and then assembled to give system equations. Once the general format of the equations of an element type (e.g. a linear distribution element) is derived, the calculation of the equations for each occurrence of that element in the body is straightforward.

7.2.2 Nodal Co ordinates

Nodal coordinates, material properties and loading conditions of the element are simply substituted into the general format. The individual element equations are assembled into the system equations, which describe the behavior of the body as a whole. For a static analysis, these generally take the form

![7.2 fem.jpg](images/00056.jpeg),

where, in structural problems, [k] is a square matrix, known as the global stiffness matrix, { δ}  is the vector of unknown nodal displacements (or temperatures in thermal analysis) and { f} is the vector of applied nodal forces (or heat flux in thermal analysis).

The equation [k] { δ}  = { f} is directly comparable to the equilibrium or load–displacement relationship for a simple one-dimensional spring we invoked previously, where a force F produces or results from a deflection u in a spring of stiffness k. To find the displacement caused by a given force, the relationship is ‘inverted’, i.e. u = k−1f.

The same approach applies to the FEM using

However, before the equation can be ‘inverted’ and solved for , some form of boundary condition must be applied, as we’ve seen. In stress problems, the body must be restrained from rigid body motion. For thermal problems, the temperature must be defined at one or more nodes. The solution to the equation is not trivial in practice because the number of equations involved tends to be very large.

It is not unreasonable to have 250 000 equations, and consequently [k] cannot be simply inverted – there is unlikely to be enough computer memory to store all the numbers and data.

  Remember that we are generally solving for the nodal displacement values first; it is then a simple matter (using a computer package) to use the displacements to find the strains and then the elemental stresses, via the appropriate Hooke’s law and strain/stress (constitutive) relations.

7.2.3 FEM Steps

The major stages in the creation of any finite element model, for most types of analysis are:

*   selection of analysis type
*   idealization of material properties
*   creation of model geometry
*   application of supports or constraints
*   application of loads
*   solution optimization.

It is extremely important to:

*   develop a feel for the behavior of the structure
*   assess the sensitivity of the results to approximations of the various types of data
*   develop an overall strategy for the creation of the model
*   compare the expected behavior of the idealized structure with the expected behavior of the real structure.

## 7.2.4 Pre-processing stage

The component under investigation is ‘discretised’ into an assembly of finite elements in the preprocessing stage, with particular reference to the following six aspects.

1.  Element boundaries should coincide with structural discontinuities.
2.  Points of application of forces (and restraints) must coincide with suitable nodes, and any abrupt changes in distributed loading must occur at element boundaries. (Pressures are applied to the centroids of element faces.)
3.  Nodes should be at the points of interest for which output data are required, e.g. displacements, reaction forces, etc.
4.  The selection of element order (e.g. linear, parabolic, cubic) defines the interpolation or shape function of displacements between nodal points, i.e. the order of a polynomial in x, y and z directions, and hence the variation of stress/strain. Furthermore, the element type (e.g. spring, rod, beam, triangular and quadrilateral planar or shell, tetrahedral or hexahedral (brick) element) needs to be chosen. Often, the expected behavior and physical shape of the component being analyzed will guide the selection.
5.  Boundary conditions (e.g. applied loads, fixed nodes and restraints) and material properties must be entered. Loads and restraints are often the most difficult parameters to represent accurately, and have a significant influence on the predictions.
6.  Extensive model checks for cohesiveness, clashes, ‘cracks’, aspect ratios of elements, etc., must be carried out.

![7.2.4 setp fem.jpg](images/00057.jpeg)

## 7.2.5 Solution stage

The fundamental unknowns to be solved are displacements u, v and, for fully three-dimensional analysis, w, for each node, with reference to a global frame of reference. Other data such as stresses and restraint reaction forces are calculated from these solution displacements, via the strains, at a later stage in the computation.

Within each element, a set of virtual displacements is applied and expressed in terms of the unknown displacements of the nodes. An element stiffness matrix is formulated using a numerical integration technique on the basis that actual displacements occurring will be those that minimize the strain energy. (This minimizing of a functional parameter as a convergence criteria is an example of the calculus of variations.)

The individual element stiffness matrices are then combined to form a global stiffness matrix for the whole body from which a vast field of linear algebraic equations relating nodal forces, element stiffness and nodal displacements are formed. Boundary conditions are applied to the relevant nodes and the displacements and are then solved using numerical techniques such as Gaussian elimination, Gauss–Seidel iteration or Cholesky square root methods. For each node connecting two or more elements, compatibility of displacements and equilibrium of forces are maintained at that node (although derivatives of the displacement interpolations generally are not continuous across interelement boundaries). The assembly of the global stiffness matrix and the solution of the displacement equations occupies most of the processing time.

Once a solution for the nodal displacements has been obtained (or in the case of iterative techniques, once satisfactory convergence is achieved for them all) for each element, the stresses are computed based on the material data entered, the original element dimensions and newly computed nodal displacements.

## 7.2.6 Post-processing stage

The results of the solution are given in the form of stress plots (e.g. maximum principal, minimum principal, maximum shear, von Mises), deformed geometry (i.e. the distorted shape) and listings of nodal displacements (ux, uy, uz). Picture files can be created to obtain hard copies, or individual programs written to read the results file and carry out further data processing, if required.

7.3.3 Triangular Elements

The simplest geometric shape that can be employed to approximate irregular surfaces is the triangle and it is one of the popular elements currently used in finite element calculations. The two-dimensional linear triangular element, also known as a simplex element, is represented by

T(x,y)=α1+α2x+α3y     7.16

7.6 Application FEM : Unsteady State Heat Transfer

Unsteady state heat flow occurs ,when the temperatures in an object change with time. The physical situation can be represented by following partial differential equation.

k( ( 2u/x2+ ( 2u/y2+ ( 2u/z2) = c( u/t)       7.39

where k is the thermal conductivity of the solid, c is its specific heat ,  is its density, u is the temperature as a function of distances in x, y z and time t.. For one dimension heat flow , it can be written as equation 7.40.

( 2u/x2 ) =k/c( u/t)

![](images/00075.jpeg)

or ( 2u/x2 ) = ( u/t)      7.40

where = k/c is thermal diffusivity.

The equation (7.40 ) is  solved for u as with variation of distance x and time by finite element method which involves a network of grid points in terms of independent variables, with grid spacing as small increment of distance x and time t

The program 7.6.1 SRP_FEM_UnsteadyState.java is given in the Appendix

Chapter 8

Application Part-I: Heat Transfer, Solidification and Casting

#### 8.1\. Cooling of large steel Slab

Temperature distribution will be computed with time for a large slab which has been removed from preheating furnace at a temperature ,say 10000 C  into slab yard with ambient temperature ,say 450 C. One of its end is insulated.

![fig8.1.jpg](images/00077.jpeg)

The problem has been illustrated in fig.8.1. The solution of temperature distribution after certain interval of time say, 20 minutes, can be found out using equation (8.1). It is often more convenient to work with dimensionless time and distance and temperature in degree Kelvin

Let us start with the following  Heat Transfer equation

Unsteady state heat flow occurs ,when the temperatures in an object change with time. The physical situation can be represented by following partial differential equation.

k( ( 2u/x2+ ( 2u/y2+ ( 2u/z2) = c( u/t)       (8.1)

where k is the thermal conductivity of the solid, c is its specific heat ,  is its density, u is the temperature as a function of distances in x, y z and time t.. For one dimension heat flow , it can be written as

( 2u/x2 ) =k/c( u/t)     (8.2)

or ( 2u/x2 ) = ( u/t)      (8.3)

where = k/c is thermal diffusivity.

The equation (8.3) is solved for u as with variation of distance x and time by finite difference method which involves a network of grid points in terms of independent variables, with grid spacing as small increment of distance x and time t (fig.8.2).

![fig8.2.jpg](images/00078.jpeg)

Here u(i+1),i is the temperature at a distance x from ui,j at a time t and u(i+1),j+1 is its value at time t + t.. Thus as we move from left to right temperature change with step increments of distance x and from bottom to time it changes with step increment of time t.

By Taylor series, one can write

u( i+1) = u(x+ x) = u(x)+(x) u(x)/x + (x)2/2! 2u(x)/x2 + (x)3/3! 3u(x)/x3+… 

Or

u( i+1) =  u(j)+(x) u(j)/x + (x)2/2! 2u(j)/x2 + (x)3/3! 3u(j)/x3+…  (8.4)

and

u( i-1) =  u(j)-(x) u(j)/x + (x)2/2! 2u(j)/x2 - (x)3/3! 3u(j)/x3+…   (8.5)

Neglecting the terms of third order or higher and solving for the first approximation to the derivative:

uj/x = (u(i+1) –u(i-1) )/2x                         (8.6)

The second derivative can be obtained in similar manner from equations (8.4) and (8.5), neglecting 4th and higher order.

2u(x)/x2 = (u( i+1) + u( i-1) - 2 u(i ) )/ (x)2               (8.7)

The derivative with respect to t is 1st order in equation (8.2) and can also be written as :

( u/t) =  (u( i), t+t  –u( i),t )/t                   (8.8)

Substituting equations    (8.6) and ( 8.7) into equation (8.2) gives

u( i), t+t =   u( i),t +  t/x2 (u( i+1),t + u( i-1),t -2 u( i), t ) …(8.9)

Using equation ( 8.9) , one can get the solution of temperature distribution in one dimension heat flow as a function of time by finite difference technique, if boundary conditions are given. It should be noted smaller the grid length x or t chosen, better will be the accuracy.

#### Boundary Conditions

The solutions also require that boundary conditions be defined at the ends points of the space interval. These may be given by mathematical relations or any fixed value.

For example , one face of the object may be insulated and heat is transferred from other face by convection or radiation or both then:

u/x= 0  , at x= L, where L is the total length

ku/x= h(ux=0 – usur ) at x=0;

where h is the overall heat transfer coefficient and usuris the temperature of the surrounding.

This will be better illustrated by considering  a real problem as follows.

. If thermal properties are assumed to remain constant , the equations are modified with dimensionless distance and time as

X= x/L,        (A)

=t/L2        (B)

and designate  temperature by T

Ti,+ = Ti,  +  /X2( Ti+1, + Ti-1, -2 Ti,) …..(8.10)

It can be shown that in order to satisfy the criteria for convergence the  ratio /x2 should be chosen less than ½.. Let us choose ¼  for the problem.

Ti,+ = ¼( Ti+1,+ 2 Ti, + Ti-1, )      ………(8.11)

The boundary condition for the problem is that heat is transfer from one end by convection and radiation and other end is insulated.

So Mathematically

            q=kT/X= h(Ts – Ti )        ……(8.12)   at X=0;

[Here dimensionless temperature Ti = (temp of  surrounding)/(temp of furnace)]

where h is the overall mass transfer coefficient for heat transfer by convection and radiation.

And at the insulated face

T/X= 0    ………(8.13)   at X=1 that is x=L

The derivative term T/X in equation (8.12) can be substituted by finite difference as explained earlier

(Ti-1, - Ti+1, )/X = h/kL(Ts – Ti) ….(8.14)   [ x/L=X ]

Combining (8.14) and (811) and substituting Ti-1,( because it falls outside the left boundary)

Ti,+ = X hL (1- Ti)/2k  + ½( Ti+1, + Ti,)    (8.15)

Similarly from equation (8.13)

(Ti-1, - Ti+1, )/X=0   and from (8.11)

Ti,+ = ¼( Ti+1,+ 2 Ti, + Ti-1, )

Substituting   Ti+1,( because it falls outside the right  boundary) we get

Ti,+  =1/2 (Ti, + Ti-1, )            (8.16)

So using equations  (8.15) and (8.16) , we find the temperatures of the two boundaries and these data fed as input into equation (8.11) to find temperature distribution at different grid points inside the slab.

If the heat transfer is by convection and conduction , the overall heat tranfer coefficient h in equation (8.15) can be obtained from equation (8.17) .

( qconvection + qradiation) =h(ux=0 – usur )= hcon(ux=0 – usur )1.25  +   (( usur)4- ( ux=0)4  (8.17)

where h is the overall heat transfer coefficient, hcon is the convective heat transfer coefficient ,  and  are constants.

We will solve the problem by writing a program in Java according to following steps.

8.1.2 Algorithm

1.  Read the input data
2.  At time t =0 , Initialize values at all grid points equal to preheating temperature.
3.  Using equation (8.14) compute temperature for next step time (+t) at one edge.
4.  Using equation (8.15) compute temperature for next step time (+t) at other edge.
5.  Feed data computed from steps 3 and 4 to equation (8.10) to find temperature distribution at the inner grid points.
6.  Repeat the steps 3, 4 and 5 for getting results  for successive next steps of time till the final time is reached.
7.  Output the computed data.

The method presented in the above section is Explicit Method because each new value of temperature  can be immediately calculated from the quantities already known. It is simple and economical to calculate but convergence and accuracy depends on value of  the  ratio  /x2 to be between 0 to ½. We can remove this limitation by going to Implicit method.

#### 8.4 Implicit Method ( Crank-Nicolson )

In this method new value of temperature is a function of unknown temperature at adjacent points.  We start from the equation (8.2),

 ( 2u/x2 ) = ( u/t)     

Here instead of forward difference , we take central difference as follows.

The derivative with respect to t is 1st order in equation  and can also be written as :

(u( i), j+1  –u( i),j )/2t =1/2[(u( i+1),j +u( i-1),j - 2 u(i ),j)/(x)2 + (u( i+1),j+1 + u( i-1), j+1  - 2 u(i ), j+1)/ (x)2]               

Central Difference at tj+1/2    Central Difference  attj           Central Difference at tj+1

The relation  /x2 =kt/(c(x)2)  

Let          r = kt/(c(x)2)   ,

Rearranging the equation , we have

-r u( i-1),j+1  + (2+2r) u(i ), j+1    -r u( i+1),j+1 = r u( i-1),j + (2-2r) u(i ),j + r u( i+1),j      (8.18)

Unlike previous explicit method where r to be chosen less than ½ for convergence, here r can have any value and hence space x and time interval t. But smaller values of r give more accurate results.

It is seen in equation (8.18) all the terms of u are at j+1 suffix in the L H S and at j in the R H S. Here j stands for time. So u values at time t +2t  in the L H S are computed from values of previous time step at t+t.

For simplification , let us consider r=1, when equation modifies to

- u( i-1),j+1  + 4u(i ), j+1   - u( i+1),j+1 =  u( i-1),j +  u( i+1),j        (8.19)

8.6 Solidification with Consideration of Latent heat of melting

We now consider  a little more complex one where both convective heat flux through liquid and conductive heat flux through the solid formed are taken into account along with  heat evolution due to solified skin formed. That is Convective heat flux through liquid - Conductive heat flux through solid =Heat evolution due to solified skin formed.

Our differential equation will be as follows.

h*(Tl[j]-Tm) -k*(Ts[j]-Tm)=d*H*dY/dt                               (8.20)

And thickness of solidified skin formed is given by

dY=(h*(Tl[j]-Tm) -ks*(Ts[j]-Tm)/Ys)*dt/(d*H)                        (8.21)

In this we need to compute the temperature of the liquid as well as solid at every instant with progress of solidification . We compute these temperatures separately and call the functions : liquidTemp( ) and  solidTemp ( ) into the main program at each differential time interval.

The program 8.6.1 SP_Solidification2.java  is given in the appendix

ForSolid

8.7 Metal Casting in metal mold

Next the solidification of a casting in mould is illustrated with just modification of the previous program. The program is given in the appendix as Program 8.7.1 SP_Casting.java

The flow of molten metal in the mold and subsequent solidification control the quality of sound casting. When a liquid metal is poured into the mold cavity, its heat is absorbed and transferred through mold wall. At the melting point , for pure metal and eutectic alloys , solidification proceeds layer by layer from the mold wall towards the hot spot region with volumetric contraction, drawing molten metal from adjacent layer, giving rise to shrinkage cavity at the sites of last layer to solidify. The moving isothermal solid-liquid interface is               called solidification front. Heat flux moves across the mold from the solidifying liquid through the  solid layer being formed. The pattern of solidification front is controlled by the temperature gradient across the mold wall. Understanding the temperature gradient and movement of solidification front helps in predicting the type and location of shrinkage defects and overcoming them successfully by better riser and gating design.

Program 8.7.1 SP_Casting.java is given in the Appendix

8.8 Metal Casting Sand Mould

![fig8.9.jpg](images/00089.jpeg)

The flow of molten metal in the mould and its subsequent solidification control the quality of sound casting. When a liquid metal is poured into the mould cavity, its heat is absorbed and transferred through mould  wall. At the melting point, for pure metal and eutectic alloys,  solidification proceeds layer by layer from the mould wall  towards the hot spot region with volumetric contraction, drawing molten metal from adjacent layer, giving rise to shrinkage cavity at the sites of last layer to solidify. The moving isothermal solid -liquid interface is called solidification front. Heat flux moves across the mould from  the solidifying liquid through the solid layer that is being  formed. The pattern of solidification front is controlled by  the temperature gradient across the mould wall. Understanding the temperature gradient and movement of  solidification front helps in predicting the type and location of shrinkage defects and overcoming them successfully by better riser and gating design.

MODEL

The present model aims at finding the temperature distribution across the mould, solid formed and liquid, The model also computes the solidification pattern with time, The equations governing unsteady state heat transfer by conduction and convections are considered. It also involves consideration of latent heat of fusion due to solidification of the liquid metal. For the time being, study will be focused on pure metal or eutectic alloys which solidify at a fixed temperature

Unsteady-state heat transfer equation by conduction is given by

(∂T/∂t) = α[(∂2T/∂x2) + [(∂2T/∂y2) +[(∂2T/∂z2)]    

where T -Temperature . t= time and α = Thermal diffusivity a=k/(cp*p)  k= thermal conductivity , cp :: specific heat, p=density

Assuming that heat transfer by conduction occurs only through wall, top and bottom of the mould being insulated, equation 1 reduces to one dimension unsteady-state heat transfer as follows.

(∂T/∂t) = α[(∂2T/∂x2)]

FOR MOULD

∂T/∂t = αm(∂2T/∂x2) ∞ < x < 0 (8.22)

where m stands for mould.

The boundary conditions here are

T=To x→-∞ [at the mould/Air interface) (8.23)

T=Ts x=O (at the mould/Solid interface) (8.24)

The temperature To is computed by balancing the heat flux by conduction through the mould with that by convection to atmosphere from the following equation :

Km (∂T/∂x)X=-∞ = h[To -Tam) (8.25)

Where Krn = Thermal conductivity of the mould h= Convective heat transfer coefficient to atmosphere

Tam = Ambient temperature

The temperature Ts , in equation (7.24) is computed by balancing the heat flux by conduction through the mould and solid from the equation (7.26).

Km (∂T/∂x)X=0- = Ks (∂T/∂x)X=0+ (8.26)

Where Ks=  Thermal conductivity of the solid

FOR SOLID

∂T/∂t = αs(∂2T/∂x2) ∞ < x < y(t) (8.27)

Where αs= Thermal diffusivity of the solid y[t)= thickness of solidified layer formed. which is a function of time.

The boundary conditions are

T=Ts x=o ( at the mould/Solid interface)

T=Tm x= y(t) ( at the Solid/Liquid interface) (8.28)

FOR LIQUID

∂T/∂t = αl  (∂2T/∂x2) y(t)  < ∞ < x (8.29)

The boundary conditions are

T=Tm x= y[t) [ at the mould/Solid interface) (8.30)

T=Tl  x→∞

Where Tl = Pouring temperature

There will be an additional boundary condition due to phase change as follows :

Ks (∂T/∂x)S - Kl (∂T/∂x)l = ps∆lH dy(t)/dt (8.31)

Where ps = density of solid

dy(t)= thickness of metal solidifies in infinitesimal time dt,

∆H= Enthalpy of the liquid which is. given by

∆H= ʃcpldt    + ∆Hf (8.32)

cpl = specific heat of liquid

∆Hf = Heat of fusion

FOR MOULD-SOLID METAL INTERFACE

An air gap is formed between the mould and solid metal interface. Maximum heat transfer takes place through this small air gap. Thickness of the air gap depends on the amount of gas generated and retained after the mould-metal reaction. the roughness of the mould surface and the expansion of the mould and cores. The heat transfer through the gap is by convection and radiation and can be expressed by following equation :

q = σέf [ (TSi  +273)4  - (Tmi +273)4] + h, (TSi - Tmi  ) (8.33))

where σ  is the Boltzman constant. έ is the emissivity and f is the form factor.

In the present model. an effective conductive heat transfer Kem of the air gap has been determined to take care overall heat transfer through the air gap by  convection and radiation, by balancing equation 8.29 with that of conductive heat transfer as follows :

Kem (∂T/∂x) = σέf [ (TSi  +273)4  - (Tmi +273)4] + h, (TSi - Tmi  ) ... (8.34)

Thus. The equations are simultaneously computed by explicit finite difference method with forward difference in time and central difference in space and subsequent computer simulations by writing programme in Java

Data Input

Program 8.7.1 SP_Casting.java ( Appendix) is run for aluminum  casting in the sand mould with the following input data

Sp Heat (L) =1090 joule/kg/deg K
Sp Heat IS) =1190 joule/kg/deg K
Density (L) =2368 Kgm/m3

Density (S) =2555 Kgm/m3  •

Thermal Conductivity (L) = 91 W Iml deg.K
Thermal Conductivity (S) = 211 W Iml deg.K
Thermal Conductivity (Sand) ;:: 0.863 W Iml deg.K
Thermal Dtffusivity IL) ;:: 3.5256e-005 -m2/set •
Thermal Diffusivity (S) '" 6.93976e-005 m2/sec
Pouring Temperature of Liquid=9630C .

Melting Point of the Metal = 933°C

Latent Heat Freezing of the Metal = -398000 Jouleslkg
Initial Temperature of Mould= 300e

Thickness of the Mould=0.2 m

Thermal Conductivity of the' Mould: 12 W Iml deg.K

Heat.Transfer Coefficient to air h, = 11.34 W 1m2 I deg.K

Output

In the finite difference method, smaller the time and space grid, the more is the accuracy. Here space grid has been taken in the order of mm and time grid in fraction of section and so number of iteration is very high. For example, for computation in 294 sees time, the programme compute and simulate all above equations for about 800 times. Figure 8.10  shows temperature distribution through the mould, solid and liquid metal. The temperature distribution of the one-half of the casting is shown in figure 8.11 It is seen that rate of temperature drop across the solid and liquid is pretty slow, while through the mould, it is very high. The maximum temperature drop occurs through the air gap by radiation and convection. Variation of temperature of the liquid and solid metal with time at various distances away from the solid-liquid interface is depicted in Fig8.11 arid Fig. 8.12 There is not much drop in temperature in solid compared to that of liquid. It is also noted here that the rate of temperature decrease is more at distances further from the interfaces. Figure 8.12 illustrates temperature distribution across the mould. It is seen here mould temperature increases with time due to flow of heat flux from the casting during solidification and the temperature decreases as it is moved away from casting-mould interface, indicating flow of heat from mould to the ambient environment. The thickness of solidified skin formation with time are displayed in Fig.8.14\. It indicates thickness formed a unit second, for example at 50th sec. how thick a solidified skin is formed from liquid. It is interesting to note that the rate of skin solidification is initially very high, that is as soon as the liquid is pored into the mould, immediately a layer of skin is formed. After this, the rate of layer formation decreases due to the fact that the heat flux now has to transfer through the solid formed

![fig8.10.jpg](images/00090.jpeg)

![fig8.14.jpg](images/00091.jpeg)

Let us consider a volume element of height dy of the ingot which has just been poured from the tundish into the primary  mold. It will have a temperature distribution with two ends being cooled by the mold and temperature increases towards the center. There will be skin of solidified layer formed near the ends in contact with mold. Due to the formation of solid , latent heat of melting will be released. 9.2 Carburization of Steel

The case depth is controlled by diffusion of carbon atoms through the surface layers of the work piece. Carbon from the surrounding atmosphere saturates the surface layer immediately after start of  case hardening process. The carbon saturation concentration at the is the maximum carbn content that can be absorbed by austenite phase to be decided by Fe-C diagram. It is about 1.3 % C at a carburizing temperature of 900 C.

![fig9.4.jpg](images/00098.jpeg)

The problem to similar to heat transfer as explained above. The difference is instead of thermal heat transfer coefficient, we consider diffusivity of carbon, instead of temperature gradient, we consider chemical potential gradient.

A program is built for Unsteady State diffusion of carbon during carburization of steel by Implicit method

With given carbon concentration at two boundaries  xboundary1, xboundary2 -  % C at the 2 boundaries

9.3 Material balance in reactor ( Blast Furnace Iron making)

The material balance in a reactor is found out by algebraic summation mass in, mass out and rate of generation or consumsption for ant specis as shown in fig9.6

![fig9.6.jpg](images/00100.jpeg)

To illustrate it, we will take an example of a popular reactor say Blast Furnace for Iron making. The table below illustrates mass in and mass out of the different specis in BF.

|  |  | Material Balance for constituents |
| Input | Output | Fe | C | O | Silica | Lime |
| Iron ore to be charged (x) | hot metal to be produced (W) | Present | Present | Present | Present |  |
| Coke to be charged (y) | Slag | Present | Present | Present | Present | Present |
| Fliux:imestone to be charged (z) | Outgoing gases  (g) |  | Present | Present | Present | Present |
| Air with O2 as oxidizer |  |  |  | Present |  |  |
|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

We can make any constituents of interest in material balance.  So there can be n no of constituents  for which we ned to form n no of equations. For simplicity, let us consider  only 3 constuents Iron, Carbon and Silica balance to establish  following 3 equations;

Iron balance

                                    α1*x+ α2*y+ α3*z=W* α4 + O* α5                   (9.1)

Silica Balance:

 β1*x+ β2*y+ β3*z= W*β4*(60/28) + O* β5+ (L*z)/B                 (9.2)                                                    (The factor 60/28 due to silicon to silica conversion)

Carbon Balance:

                              γ1*x+ γ2*y+ γ3*z=W* γ4+O* γ5                                              (9.3)

where

Percentage of Fe in iron ore (α1); Percentage Fe in coke (α2);Percentage Fe in limestone (α3)

Percentage Fe in hot metal (α4) ;Percentage Fe in off gas (α5);Percentage of Silica in iron ore (β1)

Percentage of Silica in coke (β2);Percentage of Silica in limestone (β3);Percentage of Silicon in hot metal (β4)

Percentage of Silicon in off gas (β5);Percentage of Carbon in iron ore (γ1);Percentage of Carbon in coke (γ2)

Percentage of Carbon in limestone (γ3);Percentage of Carbon in hot metal (γ4) ;Percentage of Carbon in off gas (γ5)

The Program 9.3.1  SP_MaterialBalanace.java is given in the Appendix as

The program is based on LU decomposition to find unknown solutions of n variables from n equations as discussed in chapter 2 of Numerical methods

Similarly one can develop total materials balance for all constituents of interests.

9.4 Heat and Material Balance in Rectors (BF)

The heat in and out of species  is illustrated by by the schematic diagram fig 9.7.

## ![fig9.7.jpg](images/00101.jpeg)

## The summation of all heats input and output plus exothermic and endothermic reactions are represented by the equations (9.4) and (9.5)

## Σ(Heat Content All Input) + Σ(All Exothermic)- Σ (All Endothermic)= Σ (Heat Content All output)   (9.4)

                       ΣnMiHi +   ΣnXiHi - ΣnYiHi = ΣnNiHi                     (9.5)

That is Algebraic Summation of product of Material and Heat balance in a rector is zero.

We illustrate the process by taking an example of any reactor say, blast furnace. The temperature and types of reaction in a blast furnace  varies as we move vertically from the top to the hearth as shown in fig. 8.8 . Thus for simplicity, the blast furnace is divided into three zones in which the following reactions occur:

9.4.1 Different zones in Blast furnace

Zone 1 : temperature range :(top temperature - 700C )

 haematite is reduced to magnetite.

 magnetite is heated to 700C .

 magnetite is then reduced to FeO at 700C .

 limestone is heated to 700C.

 coke is heated to 700C .

 MnO , silica ,phosphorous pentoxide, alumina are heated to 700 C .

Zone 2 : temperature range : (700C - 1200C)

 FeO is heated to 900C .

 FeO is then reduced to Fe at 900C .

 The reduced Fe is then heated to 1200C .

 limestone is heated to 1000C .

 limestone is decomposed to CaO at 1000C .

 CaO is heated to 1000C .

 coke is heated to 1200C .

 MnO , silica ,phosphorous pentoxide, alumina are heated to 1200C from700C.

Zone 3 : temperature range : (1200C-tap temperature)

 Fe{solid} is melted from 1200C to Fe{liquid} at 1200C.

 Fe{liquid} is then heated to the tap temperature.

 The metalloids are reduced at 1200C .

 Slag formation occurs.

 coke is burnt in the hearth.

![fig9.8.jpg](images/00102.jpeg)

  9.4.2 Simplifications in the above Model

i.The burden is normal as fed to iron making blast furnaces and is practically free from alumina .

ii.The limestone fed to the blast furnace is practically pure .

ii.The  coke is also practically pure and free from associated gangue and ash which is not the case in world wide practice.

iv.The percentage of direct reduction  of iron ore is zero which is also not conformable to actual practice and the sensible heat of metalloids is negligible.

v.The indirect reduction of  iron ore takes place in two distinct zones in three distinct temperature zones.

vi.The formation of eutectics between iron oxides and lime in the stack is ruled out.

vii.The slag is believed to be a mixture of various acidic and basic oxides.

viii.The slag forms at  a particular  temperature and a particular zone in the blast furnace which is a deviation from the actual practice.

ix.The indirectly reduced iron melts at a particular temperature  and in  a particular zone in the blast furnace.

x.The  use of the blast additives excepting gaseous additives other than oxygen is ruled out.

xi.The furnace is  assumed to be  in a regular state  of operation with a proper burden distribution and proper aerodynamic conditions are assumed to prevail inside the furnace.

xii.Finally other blast furnace irregularities  such as scaffolding, chilled hearth, etc should not exist in  the blast furnace.

| Constituents | Heat Input, Output & sensible heat |
| Combustion of coke |    CO: ∆Hco = ∆H298 + 298 ∫ Tb (Cp  CO - Cp  O2 - Cp  C)  dT ;   CO2 : ∆Hco2 = ∆H298 + 298 ∫ Tb (Cp  CO2 - Cp  O2 - Cp  C)  dT |

The heat and material  balance can be computed, knowing the composition of Iron ore charged, Coke, flux, hot metal and slag composition.

iΣnMiHi = 0;

The algebraic summation of molar heat, multiplied by its moles with positive  or negative sign of heat                ( whether endothermic or exothermic) will be zero. Diving blast furnace into different zones where in each zone heat and mass flow in and out can be mathematically modeled by an equation. Thus a set of linear equations can be established. A program can be written to compute the required parameters from the set of equations.

9.5 Simulation of  Reduction OF Fe2O3 BY CO/CO2 in a vertical Rector

Hematite ore is fed into the reactor and a CO/CO2 gas mixture with a fixed flow rate of U moles/sec. The gas mixture rises from the bottom through the bed of layers of different stages of iron oxide pregressively reduced, that is  Fe2O3, Fe3O4, FeO and Fe. The equilibrium gas composition at a layer is decided by the temperature of that layer.  The temperature at any layer is dependent on incoming gas temperature , the temperature of downward moving oxides / metal , heat generated by the exothermic  and endothermic reactions. The proposed model is schematically shown in fig 9.9 and 9.10

![fig9.9.jpg](images/00103.jpeg)

9.5.1 Simplification

Initially to simplify the model , we aasume , the reactions  within a zone are occurring under isothermal conditions and the wall of the reactor is fully insulated . Let us consider  any zone , say middle zone II , compute the heat and mass balance.

![fig9.10.jpg](images/00104.jpeg)

3Fe2O3(s) + CO(g) =2Fe3O4(s) + CO2(g) 

Mass in: x1 moles of Fe2O3 , x2 moles of  Fe3O4 enter the zone II and

Mass out: y1 Feo , y2 Fe3O4 leave the zone after the reaction.

The gas mixture entering the zone II has composition  CO/CO2 = q1  and the gas mixture leaving  CO/CO2 = q2 

Similarly

For Zone I

Mass in: x0 moles of Fe2O3   enter the zone I

Mass out:    x1 moles of Fe2O3 , x2 moles of  Fe3O4

Remember  x1 moles of Fe2O3 , x2 moles of  Fe3O4 are the mass out of  the zone I

For Zone III

Mass in: y1 Feo , y2 Fe3O4

Mass out: y2 Feo, z1 Fe

For Zone IV

Mass in: y2 Feo, z1 Fe

Mass out:  z2 Fe

|  | Zone I | Zone II | Zone III | Zone IV |
| Masss in | x0 Fe2O3   | x1 Fe2O3 , y1  Fe3O4 | z2 Feo , y2 Fe3O4 | z3 Feo, r3 Fe |
| Mass out | x1 Fe2O3 , y1  Fe3O4 | z2 Feo , y2 Fe3O4 | z3 Feo, r3 Fe | r4 Fe |
| Gas mixture in | c1q1 CO,  c1 CO2 | c2q2 CO,  c2 CO2 | c3q3 CO,  c3 CO2 | c4q4 CO, c4 CO2 |
| Gas mixture out | c0q0 CO,  c0 CO2 | c1q1 CO,  c1 CO2 | c2q2 CO,  c2 CO2 | c3q3 CO,  c3 CO2 |

The composition  Iron ore charged as well as iron produced are known. Gas mixture entering from the bottom and the flue gas analysis at the exit at the top  are known. We only  know iron oxide and gas mixture composition at the entry and Fe composition , gas mixture composition at exit that is zone I and IV , nothing in the intermediate zones.

Zone  I

x0 Fe2O3   + c1q1 CO +  c1 CO2 = x1 Fe2O3 + y1  Fe3O4   + c0q0 CO +   c0 CO2

zone II

x1 Fe2O3 + y1  Fe3O4   + c2q2 CO +   c2 CO2 = z2 Feo +  y2 Fe3O4 + c1q1 CO + c1 CO2

zone III

z2 Feo + y2 Fe3O4 + c3q3 CO  +  c3CO2 = z3 Feo + r3 Fe + c2q2 CO +  c2 CO2

zone IV

z3 Feo + r3 Fe + c4q4 CO + c4 CO2 = r4 Fe + c3q3 CO  +  c3 CO2

Let us replace d0= c0q0 d1= c1q1

x0 Fe2O3   + d1 CO +  c1 CO2 = x1 Fe2O3 +  y1  Fe3O4   + d0 CO +   c0 CO2

x1 Fe2O3 + y1  Fe3O4   + d2 CO +   c2 CO2 = z2 Feo +  y2 Fe3O4 + d1 CO +  c1 CO2

z2 Feo + y2 Fe3O4 + d3 CO  +  c3CO2 = z3 Feo + r3 Fe +  d2 CO +  c2 CO2

z3 Feo + r3 Fe + d4 CO + c4 CO2 = r4 Fe + z4 Feo+ d3 CO  +  c3 CO2    

| Known variables | Unknown variables |
| Solids: x0 , r4 ,  z4Gases:  C0 , C4 , d0 , d4 | Solids: x1 , y1 , y2 , z2 , z3 , r3 ,Gases: C1, C2 , C3, d1, d2 , d3, |

Another three equations can be formed from the elemental mass balance of Fe, O and C.

9.5.2 Thermodynamics  and Kinetics

Consider any zone to be under isothermal condition , the reduction reaction will take place  till equilibrium is achieved . If the equilibrium is not achieved the reaction will take place at a rate , depending on the rate controlling reaction mechanism of Gas-Solid reaction. Generally  metal oxide reduction follows either a contacting geometry topochemical reaction or 3D Grisnstling and Brounstein reaction.  The equations are given below.  From these  the amount of reduction at any zone ( during the time interval of reactants in contact with the reducing gases ) can be computed

Time Interval of contact at any zone

. The time of contact of oxide ore with reducing gas  at any zone may be estimated  from speed of downward movement of the solid , upward movement of reducing gases and the vertical distance of the zone. From the data of rate of charging the ore at the top and tap to tap time at the bottom, the time of contact can be found out.

If the temperature at any zone , rate of reduction and  time of contact at any zone are known , the amount of conversion at any zone is zone and hence x1 , y1 , y2 , z2 , z3 , r3 ,  are computed .

9.5.3 Program  Guide

Students are asked to write a program based on above model to compute the required parameters from the set of equations to study  the to reduction stages , gas composition and flow of metal and oxide  in the vertical furnace.

They should consider the following user input

Height of the furnace

Diameter of the furnace

Temperature and gas composition of  at entry and exit

Composition of iron ore charged

Composition of Direct Reduction Iron produced and oxide unreduced

Rate of Ore charged kg/hr

Rate of Iron produced kg/hr

Choice of option  of  Rate kinetics Model

Choose 1 : Contacting geometry Topochemical reaction

Choose 2 : Diffusion model Grinstling Broustein Model

Choose 3 : Chemical reaction Controlled 1st order, 2nd order etc.

Choose 4:  Not known the software decides depending on data fed

9.6   LD furnace, utilization of Heat for optimization of   scrap addition

An LD vessel is more or less an autogenous batch reactor. It takes a specified tonnage of input hot metal and refines it into steel using pure oxygen . This present work of ours tends to attempt the evaluation of thermal balance of an LD vessel taking a simplified model. computation for thermal balance is based on standard thermodynamic data such as enthalpy values of reactions, the specific heat capacity at constant pressure of various elements and compounds, etc. The program computes  the heat balance for various sizes of  inputs , operating parameters , input quality and the  quantity of scrap which can be subsequently added to improve the thermal efficiency and  productivity .

9.6.1 Simplifications and assumptions

I.) Only plain carbon steel is assumed to be produced in the LD vessel .

II.)  The refractory of the vessel does not interact with the steel melt.

III.) Lime is assumed to be supplied to the vessel in a calcined  form and is assumed to be pure and anhydrous.

IV.)  Feed of raw iron ore is ruled out.

V.) The retaining of some slag in the LD vessel during tapping is an established practice which is ruled out in this present model.

VI.)  The scrap that is fed in the vessel is also plain carbon steel scrap and is assumed to be totally unoxidized.

 9.6.2 Input Heat Sources :

         The sources of heat input to the vessel are :

*   Heat content of hot metal.
*   Oxidation heat of  Silicon in the hot metal.
*   Oxidation heat of  Carbon in the hot metal.
*   Oxidation heat of  Phosphorous in the hot metal.
*   Oxidation heat of  Manganese in the hot metal.
*   Oxidation heat of  Iron in the hot metal.

9.6.3  Output Heat Sources:

         The sources of heat output from the vessel  are :

*   Heating Steel scrap added.
*   Sensible heat of the slag.
*   Formation of CaS and other slag products.
*   Sensible heat of the LD gas

9.6.4 Heat Balance

Heat Balance

| Heat in | Heat out |

|  
# 1.)  Sensible heat of the hot metal

 |

|  
# 1) Sensible heat of the liquid steel

 |  |
| 2) Oxidation heat of Silicon in the hot metal | 2) Sensible heat of the slag |
| 3) Oxidation heat of Carbon in the hot metal | 3) Heating of lime |
| 4) Oxidation heat of  Phosphorous in the hot metal | 4) Sensible heat of the LD gas |
| 5) Oxidation heat of  Manganese in the hot |  |
| 6) Oxidation heat of Iron in the hot metal |  |
| Hin = ∆Hi + ∆Hii  + ∆Hiii  + ∆Hiv +  ∆Hv  +  ∆Hvi  | Hout=  ∆Hi + ∆Hii  + ∆Hiii  + ∆Hiv.. |

A sample program 9.6.5 SP_LDHeatBalance .java is given in the appendix. The program can be modified depending on the requirements.

Chapter 10

Application Part-III,

Structure Degradation  and Protection

10.1  Life Prediction of Corroding Steel Structures in Marine environment

Prediction of behavior of a system where mathematical relationship is not known is very important aspect of progress in applied science and engineering by computer modeling. In this case a pool of data , experimental or real are needed to predict the property or parameter from the statistics of data.

Life prediction of steel structure in marine environment is a challenging task for design and corrosion engineers for existing structures as well as new structures to be constructed. The rate of marine corrosion is a complex function of many parameters of fluid properties and chemistry of seawater. The major parameters influencing the rate are salinity, sulphate, bicarbonates, pH and temperature and dissolved oxygen. These parameters have a wide range of variation, depending on geographical locations of the structure as well as various depths of seawater in which parts of the structures are submerged. The inter-relationship among the parameters make it very difficult to establish a mathematical equation. It is also interesting to find a geographical location where rate of degradation of structure will be minimal.

10.1.1 Modeling by Genetic Algorithm (GA)

We have learned GA in the chapter 5\. It is one of the best ways to solve a problem of which little is known. An algorithm is started with a set of solutions (represented by chromosomes) called population. Solutions from one population are taken and used to form a new population to find a better one than the old one. Solutions that are selected to form new solutions (offspring) are selected according to their fitness - the more suitable they are, the more chances they have to reproduce. The algorithm consists of the following steps:

1\. Generate a random population of n chromosomes (suitable solutions for the problem)

2\. Evaluate the fitness f(x) of each chromosome x in the population

3\. Create a new population by repeating the following steps:

-Selection: Select two parent chromosomes from a population according to their fitness. In a roulette wheel selection, individuals are given a probability of being selected that is directly proportional to their fitness (the better fitness, the bigger the chance to be selected) -Crossover: With a crossover probability, cross over of the parents to form a new offspring. The most common type is single point crossover. If no crossover is performed, offspring is an exact copy of the parents.-Mutation: With a mutation probability new offspring mutate at each locus (position in chromosome). -Accepting: Place new offspring in a new population.

4\. Use a new generated population for a further run of the algorithm

5\. Test: If the end condition is satisfied, stop and return the best solution in the current population or else, (Loop) Go to step 2

A genetic algorithm is applied to obtain a best fit chromosome from the combination of five genes viz chloride, sulphate, PH, temperature and dissolved oxygen content in order to minimize the objective function (Equation 5) which represents the rate of corrosion as complex functions of all

parameters. Each gene length is a six digit coded binary number and the chrome length is a 30 digit binary. The decoded values of genes are subject to the constraints, the minimum and maximum decoded values of gene Cl- 15-24, gene SO42- 0.8 – 3.4 gene [O] 5-12, gene PH 4-8, gene temp 10-40.

The algorithm consists of the following steps:

1\. Each chromosome is tested to see how good it is in solving the problem at hand and accordingly it is assigned a fitness score. The fitness score is a measure of how good the chromosome is in minimizing objective function.

2\. There are 600 populations chosen in the program and each population consists of 2000 generations of chromosomes. Two members from the current population are selected. The chance of being selected is proportional to the chromosome fitness. A roulette wheel selection  was used. A pie chart or roulette wheel represents the total fitness score. A slice of the wheel is assigned to each member. The size is proportional to the chromosome fitness score, that is the fitter the member the bigger the size it gets.

3\. Two chromosomes at a time are taken for CROSSOVER, depending on the crossover rate. The crossover bits are selected from each chosen chromosome at a randomly chosen point. Here two chromosomes will swap their bits by selecting a random gene along the length of the chromosomes and swapping all the genes after that point. A crossover rate of 0.7 was chosen here.

![](images/00107.jpeg)

4\. Next in the mutation, bits within a chromosome will be flipped (0 becomes 1, 1 becomes 0). The mutation rate as been taken at a very low value of 0.001.

5.Steps 2, 3 and 4 are repeated, until a new population is created.

The program 10.1.1 SP_GeneticCorrosion .java is given in the Appendix

Best fit chromosome found = 010001000111000011001101000101

No. of generation: 586

Population: No. 2

Gene combinations are

Gene No. 1: (Cl-) Gene (Binary)010001 = (Decoded) 17 gm/L

Gene No. 2: (PH) Gene (Binary)000111 = (Decoded) 7

Gene No. 3: (SO42) Gene (Binary)000011 = (Decoded) gm/L

Gene No. 4: (Temp) Gene (Binary)001101 = (Decoded) 13°C

Gene No. 5 [O] Gene (Binary)000101 = (Decoded) 5 ppm

Minimum corrosion rate Icorr= 16.0225 A/cm2

It is seen from the above results that the first population of 2000 generations could not produce the required chromosome. In the second population, after 586 numbers of generations, the best gene combinations are found. Thus an aqueous marine environment of 17 gm/L chloride concentration, 3gm/L sulphate concentration and 5 ppm dissolved oxygen content having a pH of 7 at a temperature of 13°C gives a minimum corrosion rate of 16.0225 A/cm2 to a mild steel structure. Experiments were conducted with these constituents, pH and temperature and the rate of corrosion

obtained was 18.28 A/cm2

10.2  Study Corrosion Behavior of Material with Chemistry of the environment by ANN

Ocean water across the globe is corrosive but the intensity of the degradation process of the submersed metallic structures vary, depending on the geographical location of the structures. This is due to the fact that the parameters which primarily control the life of structures viz. Chloride (Cl-), Sulphate (SO42-), Bicarbonate ((HCO3-), pH, temperature (T), dissolved oxygen [O], vary across the different ocean and sea water as well as along the depth.  We will try to study the degradation rate with variation seawater composition by building a computer model by Artificial neural network (ANN).

An ANN modeling involves a set of uniformed associative neurons. “Each of them has certain informative inputs which are equal to the number of independent input parameters of the system, and a certain number of outputs, where the output parameters are formed. To design and training of neural networks, following matter must consider: (i) architecture of the neural network, (ii) training algorithm, and (iii) transfer function.

The detail of ANN modeling has been discussed in the chapter 6.The general structure of input, one hidden and one output layer was used. The numbers of neurons in the input layer and the output layer are determined by the numbers of input and output parameters, respectively” (9 ).

Data Input

Five input neurons, namely, chloride, sulphate (SO4 --), pH, and bi-carbonate (HCO3 -), temperature were taken to predict output, the corrosion current.

First we form  a matrix of input p and output q

p= [ Cl, SO4, O, pH, T ]

t= [ Icorr ]

where Cl, SO4, O, are the concentrations of chloride, sulphate and dissolved oxygen in ocean water  at a pH and temperature T

And Icorr is the rate corrosion of the degrading material in sea water , expressed in  electrochemical corrosion current

First we have to supply some known experimental or real values of p and q as follows

10.3 Modeling and simulation of Cathodic Protection

![fig10.3.jpg](images/00110.jpeg)

Potential applied for cathodic protection attenuates from the point of its application with increasing distance, giving rise to problems of overprotection and underprotection.  if protected potential at the point A (fig.10.3 )   on  a pipe, the potentials at B decreases and at C it further decreases below the required potential.  If the potential at A is increased, the region around may well be protected, but is underprotected at C and overprotected at A. 

. Complexity of interrelation among the several variables viz. chemistry, fluid properties, temperature, polarization behavior etc controlling potential attenuation does not permit a simple relation to exist. Mathematical models have been developed, based on Laplace equation, and subsequent numerical analysis and computer programming by visual C++. Models are able to find out potential distribution in some real systems.

This model will be useful to find out potential distribution of a structure, already installed and field tests or corrosion monitoring can be done to get some of the input data. The program has been
run for very big rectangular surface to study to what level potential drops down

10.3.1 Model Simplification

The model divides the electrolyte volume into a network of fine NODES whose electrical properties are related to one another by sets of equation, based on Laplace equation. So Numerical techniques and subsequent computer programming can obtain the solutions of the equations  provided some boundary conditions. are supplied.

Under the assumptions that concentration of the electrolyte is uniform, potential and current
distribution in the near a cathodic protection system, is governed by Laplace equation

∆2Φ = 0

For 2D d2Φ/ dx2  + d2Φ/ dy2  = 0

where Φ is any electrical properties of a media. The model divides the electrolyte volume into a network of fine NODES whose electrical properties are related to one another by sets of equation, based on Laplace equation. So, numerical  techniques and subsequent computer programming
can obtain the solutions of the equations provided some boundary conditions are supplied.

10.3.2 Boundary conditions

Here the boundary conditions for Laplace equation are potentials at those nodes which are at the boundary of the surface. Given these boundary values, the potential at inner nodes can be obtained by solving the Laplace equation. For cathodic protection one of the values at the boundary is applied potential either by impressed  current or sacrificial anode. To find boundary values at other outer nodes, more input data are required. These are applied current density due to corrosivity of the media (water/ soil) and its resistivity. Based on first principle based Ohm's law, the boundary value at other outer nodes are found out by following equation.

The program 10.3.3 SP_CathodicProtection.java is given in the appendix

Phase Diagram and Alloy design

11.1 Thermodynamics and Gibbs Free Energy

![11.1 ph dia.jpg](images/00113.jpeg)

A phase diagram is a graphical representation of the equilibrium state of a system using the intensive variables T and x while p is kept constant. The equilibrium may be calculated from thermodynamic data using G for

all relevant phases.

G=XA GA +XB GB +ΔHmix −TΔ Smix                                               (11.1)

For Ideal solution

Gid = XA GA +XB GB + RT( XA lnXA +XB lnXB)                               (11.2)  

For regular solution

Greg = XA GA +XB GB + ΩXA XB +  RT( XA lnXA +XB lnXB)              (11.3)  

where Ω is the coefficient of free energy excess for regular solution

![fig11.1.jpg](images/00114.jpeg)

11.2 Construction of Phase Diagram from Gibs Free Energy

The steps to generate phase diagram of Binary system A-B  are as follows

1.Get Free Energy vs. Composition diagrams for liquidus and solidus lines at different temperatures between melting point of A and B .

2\. Get slope ( chemical potential)  to curve at various composition at fixed temperature for solidus and liquidus lines

3\. Find the common tangent where the slope  to the liquidus line equates to that on solidus line.  One can get 2  points  of a phase diagram at that temperature

4\. Repeat the steps 1-3 for other points at other temperature. The full phase diagram is drawn.

Note :

The components A-B can be ideal solution or regular solution. I will shown programming for regular solutions for an Isomorphous system and Eutectic systems.

∆G  ( regular )= XAGA +XBGB +ΩXAXB + RT( XAlnXA +XBlnXB)

11.2.1 Binary Isomorphous  (Binary solutions with unlimited solubility)

The program 11.2.1 SP_PhaseDiagram.java is given in the appendix

Data Input

Melting pt Tb=1210.4 K;

Enthaply of Fusion A =50.5 Kj/mole;

Enthaply of Fusion B =36.94 Kj/mole;

For Regular solution coefficient of Heat of mixingfor solid  e1=6.5;

For Regular solution coefficient of Heat of mixingfor Liquid  e2=3.5;

![fig11.2.jpg](images/00115.jpeg)

 11.2.2 Binary Eutectic ( Binary solutions with a miscibility gap )

Let’s consider a system in which the liquid phase is approximately ideal, but for the solid phase we have

 ΔHmix > 0.

At low temperatures, there is a region where the solid solution is most stable as a mixture of two phases α1 and α2 with compositions X1 and X2\. This region is called a miscibility gap.

![fig11.3.jpg](images/00116.jpeg)

The program 11.2.1 SP_PhaseDiagram.java can be modified to take care of a  miscibility gap in the binary eutectic system. The program is run with the following data input

For Regular solution coefficient of Heat of mixing for solid  e1=0;

For Regular solution coefficient of Heat of mixing for Liquid  e2=-2000;

11.3 Alloy Design

There is a recurring need for modification or development of improved alloys and may be − Performance driven (example: increased operating temperature of exhaust valves) − Cost driven (example: lowering cost of cast irons)

Conventional trial-and-error alloy development methodology is expensive and time consuming

Path to improved alloy composition may not be obvious in complex, multi-component systems

Significant advances have been made in computational thermodynamic and kinetic modeling tools − Improved models for multi-component systems are available − Large experimental database of phase equilibria available for comparison and verification

11.3.1 Empirical Modeling

Large collection of data of alloy compositions vs. alloy properties such as strength, hardness are required for modeling and simulation which can be performed by  interpolation/extrapolation , Genetic algorithm and neural network.  The main features of this empirical modeling are as follows

• Uses existing database of relationship between material composition and its properties

• Relationships are used to identify new composition with requiredproperties

• • Curve fitting, neural networks, etc − Disadvantages

• Need Access to a large database relating inputs (composition/microstructure) to outputs (microstructure/properties)

• Extrapolation beyond available parameter space is difficult

11.3.2 Phenomenological Modelling

It is performed by understanding of physical phenomena of alloy properties controlled by microstructural design − Thermodynamic modeling − Kinetic Modeling . This has been illustrated by a schematic diagram in fig 11.5

![fig11.5.jpg](images/00118.jpeg)

11.4 Alloy Development for Automotive Applications

The steps in alloy development by modeling for an automobile  engine material has been schematically shown in fig 11.6.

![fig11.6.jpg](images/00119.jpeg)

11.5 Fracture Toughness

The unpredictable structure failures of carbon steel and low alloy steel, leading to accidents is influenced by several factors, viz. material composition, yield stress,  heat treatment, microstructures. To understand the effect of these factors on fracture toughness, millions and millions experiments are to be conducted.  Thus the alternative approach is mathematical and computer modelling using some experimental data.

Fracture toughness is a property which describes the ability of a material containing a crack to resist [fracture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fracture), and is one of the most important properties of any material for many design applications. The linear-elastic fracture toughness of a material is determined from the [stress intensity factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_intensity_factor)  at which a thin crack in the material begins to grow. It is denoted by KIc. The subscript I denotes mode I crack opening under a normal tensile stress perpendicular to the crack i.e. opening mode, since the material can be made deep enough to stand shear (mode II) or tear (mode III). Fracture toughness is a quantitative way of expressing a material's resistance to [brittle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brittle) fracture when a crack is present. If a material has high fracture toughness

 11.5.1  Stress Intensity Factor

Stress-intensity Factor (K) is a quantitative parameter of fracture toughness determining a maximum value of stress which may be applied to a specimen containing a crack (notch) of a certain length. Depending on the direction of the specimen loading and the specimen thickness, four types of stress-intensity factors are used: KC, KICKIIC KIIIC.

KC – stress-intensity factor of a specimen, thickness of which is less than a critical value. KCdepends on the specimen thickness. This condition is called plane stress. KIC,KIIC, KIIIC – stress-intensity factors, relating to the specimens, thickness of which is above the critical value therefore the values of KIC KIIC KIIIC do not depend on the specimen thickness. This condition is called plane strain.

11.5.2 Modeling by ANN

The neural network was designed with 8 input neurons, YS ,yield stress and composition of C, Si, Mn, Mo, Ni, Cr, V and one output KIC , fracture toughness, using over 100 sets of data ( Appendix-2) to study the effect of  composition and yield stress on fracture toughness, (with two hidden layers, 20 neurons in the first hidden layer, 1 neuron in the 2nd hidden layer)   The learning rate is 0.05; ,no of epochs = 500 and error goal = 10-5.

Program

The program 11.5.2  SP_Fracture.m has been coded in Matlab not in Java, as Matlab code for this application is much more simple and the code is given in Appendix. The fracture toughness data used for the program is given in table A1 in appendix.

 import java.util.Scanner;
 import java.io.*;

 public  class SP_Progm1
    {
 public  static  void main(String[] args)

        {
 //for reading array
Scanner inpData = new Scanner(System.in);

            System.out.print("Enter the dimension of the room l,w,h, 

       consider w is less than l & h is less than w,h shortest ");

 double l = inpData.nextDouble();
 double w = inpData.nextDouble();
 double h = inpData.nextDouble();

      System.out.print("Enter the smallest ball dia u would consider to

       start with ");

 float x=inpData.nextFloat();

  System.out.print("increanment of ball dia each stage by fraction of    

    intial dia say, 0.20, 0.30 etc");
 float y=inpData.nextFloat();

     System.out.print("Enter maximum  no of times,u want to increse the

      ball dia");
 int n = inpData.nextInt();

 double d[]= new  double[n];
 double v[]= new  double[n];
 double V[]= new  double[n];

 int nm[]= new  int [n];

    System.out.print("Enter the smallest ball dia u would consider to

     mstart with ");

      d[1]=x;
 int k=1;
 for(int i=1;i<=n;i++)

    {      
      v[i] =(float)(Math.PI*(Math.pow(d[i],2.0))/4) ;

       nm[i]=(int)((l*w*h)/v[i]);

       V[i]= l*w*h- nm[i]*v[i];
       d[i+1]=d[i] + y*d[i];// increasing diameter of the ball by 20 %
       k=i;
 if (d[i+1]>h) break;
        }
  //Display data
    System.out.println("k,n," +k +n  );

 for(int i=1;i<=k;i++)
     {      
         System.out.print("dia= "+(int)d[i]);

         System.out.print("  number= "+nm[i]);

         System.out.println("  vacant space=" +(float)V[i] );

      }

     }                
    }
